=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a length-N sequence A = (A_1, A_2, \ldots, A_N).
You will repeatedly perform the following operation until the sequence has length at most 1: choose two adjacent numbers and remove both from the sequence.
The score obtained in one operation is the absolute difference of the two chosen numbers.
Find the maximum possible total score obtained.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \ldots A_N

Output

Print the maximum possible total score obtained.

Constraints


- 2 \le N \le 3 \times 10^5
- 1 \le A_i \le 10^9
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

4
1 2 5 3

Sample Output 1

5

First, remove A_2 and A_3. The score obtained is |A_2 - A_3| = 3.
Next, remove A_1 and A_4. Note that, because of the previous operation, these two numbers are now adjacent. The score obtained is |A_1 - A_4| = 2.
Hence, the total score obtained is 5.
It is impossible to achieve a total score of 6 or greater, so print 5.

Sample Input 2

7
3 1 4 1 5 9 2

Sample Output 2

14

Sample Input 3

5
1 1 1 1 1

Sample Output 3

0

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Thus problem reduces to computing:
If N even:
sort A, answer = sum largest N/2 - sum smallest N/2.

If N odd:
sort A. Need max over i (omitted index) of (sum of k largest among all except i) - (sum of k smallest among all except i), where k=(N-1)/2.
Need compute efficiently O(N log N) or O(N). N 3e5, sorting fine.

Need derive formula for odd. Let sorted B[0..N-1] ascending, N=2k+1. For omitted index i:
Remaining N-1=2k elements. The k largest among remaining are:
- If i < k? Let's think. Without omission, k largest are indices N-k .. N-1 (last k), k smallest are indices 0..k-1 (first k). There is a middle element at index k (since N=2k+1, indices 0..2k; first k indices 0..k-1, middle index k, last k indices k+1..2k). Actually sorted: [small k], median, [large k].
If omit one element, the k largest of remaining and k smallest of remaining depend on whether omitted in small, middle, or large.

Let's define:
S_small = sum B[0..k-1] (k smallest original)
S_large = sum B[k+1..2k] (k largest original)
M = B[k] (median).

If omit from small half (i < k): Remaining small count k-1, large count k, plus median. Need choose k smallest among remaining: likely all original small except omitted plus median? Let's calculate. Remaining elements: small set without i (k-1 elements), median M, large set (k elements). Sorted. The k smallest among remaining are the k-1 remaining small elements plus M? Since M ≤ all large, and > all small? Actually M ≥ all small (sorted) and ≤ all large. So yes, k smallest = (small \ {i}) ∪ {M}. Sum = S_small - B[i] + M. The k largest among remaining are the k large elements (since M ≤ large, and small remaining ≤ M ≤ large). Sum = S_large. So signed sum = S_large - (S_small - B[i] + M) = (S_large - S_small - M) + B[i].

If omit from large half (i > k): Remaining large count k-1, small count k, median. k largest among remaining = (large \ {i}) ∪ {M}. Sum = S_large - B[i] + M. k smallest = S_small. Signed sum = (S_large - B[i] + M) - S_small = (S_large - S_small + M) - B[i].

If omit median (i = k): Remaining small k, large k. k smallest = S_small, k largest = S_large. Signed sum = S_large - S_small. This equals both formulas with B[i]=M? For i<k formula S_large - S_small - M + B[i] = S_large - S_small. For i>k formula S_large - S_small + M - B[i] = S_large - S_small. Good.

So max over i:
max(
 max_{i<k} [C + B[i]] where C = S_large - S_small - M,
 i=k: S_large - S_small,
 max_{i>k} [D - B[i]] where D = S_large - S_small + M
)
Since B sorted ascending, for i<k, C+B[i] maximized at i=k-1 (largest small). For i>k, D-B[i] maximized at i=k+1 (smallest large). Also median option.

Let's simplify:

Median = S_large - S_small.

D-B[k+1] = base - (B[k+1] - M) ≤ base.

Alternative simpler: add to lower, then move max to upper, then if upper > lower move min to lower. Let's use robust.

Pseudo:
def prefix_scores(arr):
    n=len(arr)
    scores=[0]*(n+1)
    lower=[] # max heap negatives
    upper=[] # min heap
    sum_low=sum_up=0
    for i,x in enumerate(arr):
        # push to lower
        heappush(lower, -x); sum_low += x
        # move largest from lower to upper
        val = -heappop(lower); sum_low -= val
        heappush(upper, val); sum_up += val
        # if upper size > lower size, move smallest from upper to lower
        if len(upper) > len(lower):
            val = heappop(upper); sum_up -= val
            heappush(lower, -val); sum_low += val
        # Now lower size = ceil((i+1)/2), upper = floor. After even total, equal.
        if (i+1)%2==0:
            scores[i+1] = sum_up - sum_low
    return scores

Need implement prefix_scores efficiently. Let's detail.

Function prefix_scores(arr):
    n = len(arr)
    scores = [0]*(n+1)
    import heapq
    lower = []  # max heap via negative
    upper = []  # min heap
    sum_low = 0
    sum_up = 0
    for i, x in enumerate(arr):
        # insert into lower
        heapq.heappush(lower, -x)
        sum_low += x
        # move largest from lower to upper
        v = -heapq.heappop(lower)
        sum_low -= v
        heapq.heappush(upper, v)
        sum_up += v
        # if upper has more elements than lower, move smallest from upper to lower
        if len(upper) > len(lower):
            v = heapq.heappop(upper)
            sum_up -= v
            heapq.heappush(lower, -v)
            sum_low += v
        if (i+1) % 2 == 0:
            scores[i+1] = sum_up - sum_low
    return scores

Let's verify heap invariant with this algorithm for all cases. At each step:
- Push x to lower, then pop max from lower to upper. This ensures all elements in lower <= all in upper? Let's check. Before insertion, lower contains smaller half, upper larger half, with lower max <= upper min. After push x to lower, lower may contain x larger than upper min? Need ensure prefix_scores function uses heapq imported. In final code, include import heapq and sys.stdin.buffer.

Let's write code:

import sys, heapq

def prefix_scores(arr):
    n = len(arr)
    scores = [0] * (n + 1)
    lower = []  # max heap, store negative
    upper = []  # min heap
    sum_low = 0
    sum_up = 0
    for i, x in enumerate(arr):
        heapq.heappush(lower, -x)
        sum_low += x

        v = -heapq.heappop(lower)
        sum_low -= v
        heapq.heappush(upper, v)
        sum_up += v

        if len(upper) > len(lower):
            v = heapq.heappop(upper)
            sum_up -= v
            heapq.heappush(lower, -v)
            sum_low += v

        if (i + 1) % 2 == 0:
            scores[i + 1] = sum_up - sum_low
    return scores

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data: return
    N = data[0]
    A = data[1:]
    if N % 2 == 0:
        A.sort()
        k = N // 2
        ans = sum(A[k:]) - sum(A[:k])
        print(ans)
    else:
        pref = prefix_scores(A)
        ans = pref[N - 1]  # omit the last element
        lower = []
        upper = []
        sum_low = 0
        sum_up = 0
        # process reversed A
        for L, x in enumerate(reversed(A), 1):
            heapq.heappush(lower, -x)
            sum_low += x

            v = -heapq.heappop(lower)
            sum_low -= v
            heapq.heappush(upper, v)
            sum_up += v

            if len(upper) > len(lower):
                v = heapq.heappop(upper)
                sum_up -= v
                heapq.heappush(lower, -v)
                sum_low += v

            if L % 2 == 0:
                suff = sum_up - sum_low
                left_len = N - 1 - L
                cand = pref[left_len] + suff
                if cand > ans:
                    ans = cand
        print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Now, let's revisit the proof of odd split: Is it true that no pair can enclose the unmatched element? Suppose pair (i,j) with i<u<j. To remove i and j together, they must become adjacent at some time. That requires all elements between them removed before. Since u is between and never removed, impossible. Thus no pair encloses u. Therefore all pairs are entirely left or right of u. But what about a pair (i,j) with i<u and j<u but another pair (p,q) with p>u? They are independent. Good.

Now, let's consider if the unmatched element could be at an odd 0-based position but left/right lengths odd, yet maybe one pair crosses over u after u is "skipped"? No, u remains, cannot be skipped. So no.

Now, let's think about if F(segment) formula for even segment with no unmatched is always feasible within the larger sequence when there is an unmatched outside. Suppose we choose u even, left segment length even. We can match left segment optimally using operations entirely within left segment. Similarly right. Then after left and right reduced to empty, u remains. Are operations in left and right independent? Yes, because u separates them; operations in left don't affect adjacency in right except u? Actually if we perform operations in left, the rightmost element of left may become adjacent to u, but we won't pair with u; we can pair within left before touching right. We can process left fully, then right fully, or interleave. Since left and right are separated by u, operations within left never involve elements to the right of u because u is between; but after some left removals, the remaining left elements are still left of u, and the right segment remains right of u. They are not adjacent to each other because u is between. So independent. Good.

Now, let's verify the sign assignment lower bound for even segment in more detail:
Given segment values, choose + on largest half, - on smallest half. Need construct a matching where every pair has one + and one -, and every + value >= every - value. We argued any sequence with equal + and - can be paired by repeatedly removing adjacent opposite signs. But does this process always pair every + with a -? Yes, because at each step we remove one + and one -, counts remain equal; eventually empty. The removed pairs are adjacent at time of removal, so valid. The score of each pair = A_+ - A_- (since A_+ >= A_-) = |diff|. Sum = sum_+ - sum_- = F. Thus lower bound.

Upper bound for even segment:
Any matching has pairs. For each pair (i,j), |A_i-A_j| = max_{s_i=-s_j} (s_i A_i+s_j A_j). Let choose for each pair the sign of larger as +, smaller as -. This gives a global sign assignment with equal numbers of + and - (one per pair). The score equals sum s_i A_i. Since any balanced sign assignment has sum ≤ sum largest half - sum smallest half, score ≤ F. Good.

For odd segment:
Upper bound: Any matching leaves u. Pairs left/right. Let left score ≤ F(left), right score ≤ F(right) by even upper bound. Thus total ≤ max over feasible u of F(left)+F(right). Lower bound: For u feasible, match left optimally and right optimally; total = F(left)+F(right). So answer = max. Good.

Now, let's consider if F(left)+F(right) can be computed by prefix/suffix scores. F(prefix length L) for even L. Yes.

Potential issue: In odd branch, we compute prefix_scores(A) for full A, but F(prefix length L) for L even. The heap algorithm's scores[L] is F of first L elements. Good. Suffix on reversed A gives F of last L elements. Good.

Let's draft final code:

import sys
from heapq import heappush, heappop

def prefix_scores(arr):
    n = len(arr)
    scores = [0] * (n + 1)
    lower = []  # max heap (store negated values)
    upper = []  # min heap
    sum_low = 0
    sum_up = 0
    for i, x in enumerate(arr):
        heappush(lower, -x)
        sum_low += x

        v = -heappop(lower)
        sum_low -= v
        heappush(upper, v)
        sum_up += v

        if len(upper) > len(lower):
            v = heappop(upper)
            sum_up -= v
            heappush(lower, -v)
            sum_low += v

        if (i + 1) % 2 == 0:
            scores[i + 1] = sum_up - sum_low
    return scores

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N = data[0]
    A = data[1:]
    if N % 2 == 0:
        A.sort()
        k = N // 2
        ans = sum(A[k:]) - sum(A[:k])
        print(ans)
        return

    pref = prefix_scores(A)
    ans = pref[N - 1]  # leave the last element unmatched

    lower = []
    upper = []
    sum_low = 0
    sum_up = 0

    for L, x in enumerate(reversed(A), 1):
        heappush(lower, -x)
        sum_low += x

        v = -heappop(lower)
        sum_low -= v
        heappush(upper, v)
        sum_up += v

        if len(upper) > len(lower):
            v = heappop(upper)
            sum_up -= v
            heappush(lower, -v)
            sum_low += v

        if L % 2 == 0:
            suff = sum_up - sum_low
            left_len = N - 1 - L
            cand = pref[left_len] + suff
            if cand > ans:
                ans = cand

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Now, let's think about if the heap algorithm's invariant "all lower <= all upper" is maintained by the standard add-to-lower-then-move-max-to-upper-then-rebalance. Let's prove to ensure no bug:
Invariant before each iteration: lower and upper are sorted heaps, all elements in lower <= all in upper, and len(lower) = len(upper) if processed even, or len(lower)=len(upper)+1 if processed odd.
Add x to lower. Now lower may contain x larger than some upper. Move max from lower to upper. Let m = max(lower ∪ {x}). After moving m to upper, all remaining lower elements <= m, and m <= all upper? All lower <= upper yes. In general, moving max from lower to upper: the moved element m is >= all remaining lower. Need m <= all upper after insertion? If m was previous lower max and x <= m, then m <= previous upper min (invariant), and x <= m, so m <= upper min. If m = x and x > previous lower max, then x might be > previous upper min. Example lower [1], upper [50], add x=100. m=100, move to upper -> upper [50,100], lower [1]. Now lower <= upper? 1 <=50 yes. But m=100 is not <= all upper (50 <100). The upper heap contains 50 and100; min is50. The invariant "all lower <= all upper" only requires lower max <= upper min, not moved element <= all upper. The upper heap is not necessarily sorted by insertion but heap property ensures min at root. It's okay that 100 >50 inside upper; upper contains larger half as a multiset, not necessarily sorted. The condition for contribution is all lower <= all upper, i.e., max lower <= min upper. Here max lower=1 <= min upper=50. Good. So moving max from lower to upper works because the moved element is at least all remaining lower, but upper may contain smaller elements? Wait if upper contains 50 and moved 100, min upper 50. Is it guaranteed that all remaining lower <= min upper? Remaining lower <= moved element m. But m might be > min upper, so remaining lower could be > min upper? Let's check example lower [1], upper [50], add x=100. Remaining lower [1], min upper 50, 1<=50. What if lower [40], upper [50], add x=100. m=100, remaining lower [40], min upper50, 40<=50. Adding m (>= previous lower max) to upper, min upper remains previous upper min (unless m smaller, but m >= previous lower max and previous lower max <= previous upper min, so m could be smaller than previous upper min? If m=previous lower max, m <= previous upper min, so min upper could be m if m < previous upper min. Example lower [1], upper [100], add x=0, m=1, move1 to upper -> upper [1,100], min upper1. Remaining lower [0], okay. So min upper may become m, which is >= remaining lower. Thus invariant holds. Good.

Let's see if the two-heap algorithm can be simplified to exactly 2 heap operations per element:
Maintain lower max-heap and upper min-heap. To add x:
- If lower is empty or x <= -lower[0]: push x to lower
- else: push x to upper
- Rebalance: if len(lower) > len(upper)+1: move max lower to upper; elif len(upper) > len(lower): move min upper to lower.
This uses at most 2 pushes + 1 pop + 1 pop = 4 ops, but sometimes fewer. The previous algorithm always does push lower, pop lower, push upper, and maybe pop upper = 3-4 ops. The conditional version may be better on average but still similar. Need ensure invariant. Let's design conditional version carefully:

Initialize lower=[], upper=[], sum_low=sum_up=0.
For x:
    if not lower or x <= -lower[0]:
        heappush(lower, -x); sum_low += x
    else:
        heappush(upper, x); sum_up += x
    # Rebalance to maintain lower size = ceil(total/2), upper=floor(total/2), and all lower <= upper.
    if len(lower) > len(upper) + 1:
        v = -heappop(lower); sum_low -= v
        heappush(upper, v); sum_up += v
    elif len(upper) > len(lower):
        v = heappop(upper); sum_up -= v
        heappush(lower, -v); sum_low += v
    if total even: score = sum_up - sum_low

Will this maintain all lower <= upper? Let's test with lower [1], upper [100], add x=50. x <= -lower[0]? 50<=1 false, push upper -> upper [50,100]. Sizes lower1 upper2, rebalance upper>lower: pop min upper50 to lower -> lower [1,50], upper[100]. Good.

This conditional version uses one push, then at most one pop+push. So 1-3 heap ops per element, better. Let's use this in both prefix_scores and suffix loop to improve performance. Need be careful with duplicates and comparison.

Let's rewrite prefix_scores with conditional version:

def prefix_scores(arr):
    n = len(arr)
    scores = [0]*(n+1)
    lower = []  # max heap, negatives
    upper = []  # min heap
    sum_low = sum_up = 0
    for i, x in enumerate(arr):
        if not lower or x <= -lower[0]:
            heappush(lower, -x)
            sum_low += x
        else:
            heappush(upper, x)
            sum_up += x

        if len(lower) > len(upper) + 1:
            v = -heappop(lower)
            sum_low -= v
            heappush(upper, v)
            sum_up += v
        elif len(upper) > len(lower):
            v = heappop(upper)
            sum_up -= v
            heappush(lower, -v)
            sum_low += v

        if (i+1) % 2 == 0:
            scores[i+1] = sum_up - sum_low
    return scores

Now, let's think about if for odd N, the upper bound F(left)+F(right) uses F for each even segment, which itself is based on sign assignment top half of that segment. But in an arbitrary feasible matching, the signs chosen optimally for each pair in left segment may not correspond to top half of the entire left segment? Wait, for any matching of left segment, score = sum over pairs |diff|. We can choose signs per pair (larger +, smaller -) to get a balanced sign assignment on left segment. Now, let's consider if there is any possibility of the answer for odd N being computed by prefix_scores and suffix_scores but if the optimal unmatched position u is even and left_len/
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
# The solution is:
```python
import sys
from heapq import heappush, heappop


def prefix_scores(arr):
    n = len(arr)
    scores = [0] * (n + 1)

    # lower: max-heap (store negative values) for the smaller half
    # upper: min-heap for the larger half
    lower = []
    upper = []
    sum_low = 0
    sum_up = 0

    for i, x in enumerate(arr):
        if not lower or x <= -lower[0]:
            heappush(lower, -x)
            sum_low += x
        else:
            heappush(upper, x)
            sum_up += x

        if len(lower) > len(upper) + 1:
            v = -heappop(lower)
            sum_low -= v
            heappush(upper, v)
            sum_up += v
        elif len(upper) > len(lower):
            v = heappop(upper)
            sum_up -= v
            heappush(lower, -v)
            sum_low += v

        # After processing i+1 elements, if the length is even, record score.
        if i & 1:
            scores[i + 1] = sum_up - sum_low

    return scores


def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return

    N = data[0]
    A = data[1:]
    del data

    if N % 2 == 0:
        A.sort()
        k = N // 2
        ans = sum(A[k:]) - sum(A[:k])
        print(ans)
        return

    # Odd N: one element remains unmatched.
    # It must be at an even 0-indexed position, splitting the array into
    # two even-length parts.  Each even-length part is solved independently.
    pref = prefix_scores(A)

    # Leave the last element unmatched.
    ans = pref[N - 1]

    # Compute suffix scores from right to left and combine with prefix scores.
    lower = []
    upper = []
    sum_low = 0
    sum_up = 0

    for L, x in enumerate(reversed(A), 1):
        if not lower or x <= -lower[0]:
            heappush(lower, -x)
            sum_low += x
        else:
            heappush(upper, x)
            sum_up += x

        if len(lower) > len(upper) + 1:
            v = -heappop(lower)
            sum_low -= v
            heappush(upper, v)
            sum_up += v
        elif len(upper) > len(lower):
            v = heappop(upper)
            sum_up -= v
            heappush(lower, -v)
            sum_low += v

        if (L & 1) == 0:
            suff = sum_up - sum_low
            left_len = N - 1 - L
            cand = pref[left_len] + suff
            if cand > ans:
                ans = cand

    print(ans)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
